To evaluate the eigenvalues of
Eq.

215
, we invoke the theory of finite frames
[
han2000frames
,
waldron2018introduction
]
. We begin with the definition of a frame.

Definition 0.8
.

Let
V
be a Hilbert space with inner product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
. A
frame
is a sequence
\{x_{j}\}_{j}\subset V
which satisfies

\alpha\|v\|_{V}^{2}\leq\sum_{j}|\langle x_{j},v\rangle|^{2}\leq\beta\|v\|_{V}^{2}\quad\forall v\in V,

(216)

for some real constants
\alpha,\beta>0
(called the
frame bounds
). Here,
\|v\|_{V}\coloneqq\sqrt{\langle v,v\rangle}
. A frame is called
tight
if
\alpha=\beta
.

Recall that
\det[Q_{\bm{\sigma},\bm{\tau}}]
defines the matrix elements of the orthogonal representation
\phi_{2k}\colon\mathrm{SO}(2n)\to\mathrm{SO}\mathopen{}\left[\binom{2n}{2k}\right]\mathclose{}
. In order to demonstrate the optimality of our ensemble, we shall generalize
Eq.

215
to take the average over any subgroup
G\subseteq\mathrm{Sym}^{+}(2,2n)
, in which case we consider the restricted representations
\phi_{2k}\big|_{G}\colon G\to\mathrm{SO}\mathopen{}\left[\binom{2n}{2k}\right]\mathclose{}
. (Whenever the context is clear, we shall simply write
\phi_{2k}
.) To simplify notation, we take
\mathbb{R}^{\binom{2n}{2k}}
as our representation space, spanned by the standard basis
\{e_{\bm{\mu}}\mid\bm{\mu}\in\mathcal{C}_{2n,2k}\}
.

Consider the group orbit

\phi_{2k}(G)e_{\bm{\sigma}}\coloneqq\{\phi_{2k}(Q)e_{\bm{\sigma}}\mid Q\in G\}.

(217)

A sufficient condition for the eigenvalues of
\mathcal{M}_{G}
to be nonzero (hence guaranteeing the existence of
\mathcal{M}_{G}^{-1}
) is that
\phi_{2k}(G)e_{\bm{\sigma}}
be a frame for some
\bm{\sigma}\in\mathcal{D}_{2n,2k}
. Indeed, assuming the frame condition, we have

\begin{split}\E_{Q\sim G}|{\det[Q_{\bm{\sigma},\bm{\tau}}]}|&=\frac{1}{|G|}\sum_{Q\in G}|{\det[Q_{\bm{\sigma},\bm{\tau}}]}|^{2}\\
&=\frac{1}{|G|}\sum_{Q\in G}|\langle\phi_{2k}(Q)^{\mathsf{T}}e_{\bm{\sigma}},e_{\bm{\tau}}\rangle|_{2}^{2}\\
&\geq\frac{\alpha}{|G|}\|e_{\bm{\tau}}\|_{2}^{2}>0\end{split}

(218)

for all
\bm{\tau}\in\mathcal{C}_{2n,2k}
. Note that the transpose is irrelevant since
G
is a group and
\phi_{2k}
an orthogonal representation. While
\mathcal{M}_{G}
may be made positive even without taking
G
to be a group, the group structure has desirable implications for the measurement complexity (shadow norm) of our scheme. (It is also generally easier to sample from a well-established group like the symmetric group, rather than some ad hoc subset of
\mathrm{Sym}^{+}(2,2n)
.) To see this, we shall focus attention exclusively to tight frames, which is motivated by the following observation.

Lemma 0.9
.

Let
G\subseteq\mathrm{Sym}^{+}(2,2n)
be a subset such that
\phi_{2k}(G)e_{\bm{\sigma}}
is a frame for all
\bm{\sigma}\in\mathcal{D}_{2n,2k}
,
1\leq k\leq n
. Let
\alpha_{k},\beta_{k}>0
be the cumulative frame bounds over all
\bm{\sigma}
for each
k
, i.e.,

\alpha_{k}\leq\frac{1}{|G|}\sum_{\bm{\sigma}\in\mathcal{D}_{2n,2k}}\sum_{Q\in G}|{\det[Q_{\bm{\sigma},\bm{\tau}}]}|^{2}\leq\beta_{k}

(219)

for all
\bm{\tau}\in\mathcal{C}_{2n,2k}
. Then the shadow norm associated with the ensemble
G
satisfies

\alpha_{k}/\beta_{k}^{2}\leq\|\Gamma_{\bm{\tau}}\|_{G}^{2}\leq\beta_{k}/\alpha_{k}^{2}.

(220)

These bounds are saturated if and only if the cumulative frame is tight, i.e.,
\|\Gamma_{\bm{\tau}}\|_{G}^{2}=\alpha_{k}^{-1}
.

Remark 0.10
.

In
Eq.

219
, and hence in
Eq.

215
, the actual frame being used is
\phi_{2k}(G)(e_{\bm{\sigma}}|G|^{-1/2})
, i.e., the “orbit” of the basis vector scaled by a factor of
|G|^{-1/2}
. (We use scare quotes to indicate that, since we do not require
G
to be a group here, the corresponding set of vectors may not be an orbit proper.)

Proof.

First, we recall the definition of the shadow norm:

\|\Gamma_{\bm{\tau}}\|_{G}^{2}=\max_{\text{states }\rho}\mathopen{}\left(\mathbb{E}_{Q\sim G}\mathopen{}\left[\sum_{z\in\{0,1\}^{n}}\langle z|U(Q)\rho U(Q)^{\dagger}|z\rangle\langle z|U(Q)\mathcal{M}^{-1}(\Gamma_{\bm{\tau}})U(Q)^{\dagger}|z\rangle^{2}\right]\mathclose{}\right)\mathclose{}.

(221)

Adapting
Eq.

215
to the language of frames yields the inequalities

\beta_{k}^{-1}\leq\|\mathcal{M}_{G}^{-1}(\Gamma_{\bm{\tau}})\|\leq\alpha_{k}^{-1},

(222)

where we have used the fact that the spectral norm
\|\cdot\|
of the Majorana operators is
1
. Thus

\begin{split}\|\Gamma_{\bm{\tau}}\|_{G}^{2}&\leq\alpha_{k}^{-2}\max_{\text{states }\rho}\mathopen{}\left(\mathbb{E}_{Q\sim G}\mathopen{}\left[\sum_{z\in\{0,1\}^{n}}\langle z|U(Q)\rho U(Q)^{\dagger}|z\rangle\langle z|U(Q)\Gamma_{\bm{\tau}}U(Q)^{\dagger}|z\rangle^{2}\right]\mathclose{}\right)\mathclose{}\\
&=\alpha_{k}^{-2}\max_{\text{states }\rho}\mathopen{}\left(\mathbb{E}_{Q\sim G}\mathopen{}\left[\sum_{z\in\{0,1\}^{n}}\langle z|U(Q)\rho U(Q)^{\dagger}|z\rangle\mathopen{}\left(\sum_{\bm{\mu}\in\mathcal{C}_{2n,2k}}\det[(Q^{\mathsf{T}})_{\bm{\tau},\bm{\mu}}]\langle z|\Gamma_{\bm{\mu}}|z\rangle\right)^{2}\mathclose{}\right]\mathclose{}\right)\mathclose{}.\end{split}

(223)

Let us examine the innermost bracketed term. Since
\langle z|\Gamma_{\bm{\mu}}|z\rangle=\pm 1
when
\bm{\mu}\in\mathcal{D}_{2n,2k}
and
0
otherwise, we can restrict the sum to run over
\mathcal{D}_{2n,2k}
. Furthermore, by
Lemma

0.6
, we know that
\det[(Q^{\mathsf{T}})_{\bm{\tau},\bm{\mu}}]
is nonzero only for one particular
\bm{\mu}
, which may or may not lie in
\mathcal{D}_{2n,2k}
. This means that the sum contains at most one nonzero term, which takes the value
\pm 1
, allowing us to “transform” the square into an absolute value:

\mathopen{}\left(\sum_{\bm{\mu}\in\mathcal{C}_{2n,2k}}\det[(Q^{\mathsf{T}})_{\bm{\tau},\bm{\mu}}]\langle z|\Gamma_{\bm{\mu}}|z\rangle\right)^{2}\mathclose{}=\sum_{\bm{\mu}\in\mathcal{D}_{2n,2k}}|{\det[Q_{\bm{\mu},\bm{\tau}}]}|.

(224)

Importantly, this quantity does not depend on
z
, which makes the sum over
z
trivial (as well as the maximum over all states):

\sum_{z\in\{0,1\}^{n}}\langle z|U(Q)\rho U(Q)^{\dagger}|z\rangle=\tr\rho=1.

(225)

We are then left with taking the average over
Q\sim G
in
Eq.

224
, which is simply the original quantity of interest obeying the frame bounds [
Eq.

219
]. Therefore

\begin{split}\|\Gamma_{\bm{\tau}}\|_{G}^{2}&\leq\alpha_{k}^{-2}\E_{Q\sim G}\mathopen{}\left[\sum_{\bm{\mu}\in\mathcal{D}_{2n,2k}}|{\det[Q_{\bm{\mu},\bm{\tau}}]}|\right]\mathclose{}\\
&\leq\alpha_{k}^{-2}\beta_{k}.\end{split}

(226)

To obtain the lower bound, simply reverse the roles of
\alpha_{k}
and
\beta_{k}
. Since the only inequalities invoked were those of the frame bounds, it follows that equality holds in both directions if and only if the frame is tight.
∎

Lemma

0.9
is useful in two ways: first, it gives an estimate on the shadow norm for any valid subset of fermionic Gaussian Clifford unitaries, assuming one has bounds on the eigenvalues of
\mathcal{M}_{G}
. Second, it tells us that those subsets which give rise to
tight
frames exhibit optimal sample complexity in the sense of the shadow norm. Tight frames generated by the action of finite groups have been completely characterized through representation theory
[
vale2004tight
,
waldron2018introduction
]
. Below we restate the primary results relevant to our context, which will motivate us to restrict
G
to a group.

Proposition 0.11

(
[
vale2004tight
, Theorems 6.3 and 6.5]
)
.

Let
H
be a finite group,
V
a Hilbert space, and
\varphi\colon H\to\mathrm{U}(V)
a unitary representation. Then:

1.

Every orbit
\varphi(H)v
,
v\in V\setminus\{0\}
, is a tight frame if and only if every orbit spans
V
[i.e.,
\varphi
is an irreducible representation (irrep)].

2.

There exists
v\in V\setminus\{0\}
for which
\varphi(H)v
is a tight frame if and only if there exists
w\in V\setminus\{0\}
such that
\spn(\varphi(H)w)=V
.

In our context, this means that if
\phi_{2k}\big|_{G}
is irreducible, then every orbit is tight, hence saturating the bound of
Lemma

0.9
. Alternatively, if
\phi_{2k}\big|_{G}
is not an irrep, there may be only specific orbits which form tight frames. Fortunately, this complication does not arise in our setting due to the fact that we are dealing exclusively with (signed) permutation matrices. We formalize this notion with the following lemma. (Although we are only interested in the even-degree representations, we formulate the statement to apply to all
1\leq k\leq 2n
for completeness.)

Lemma 0.12
.

Let
G\subseteq\mathrm{Sym}^{+}(2,2n)
be a group such that
\spn(\phi_{k}(G)v_{0})=\mathbb{R}^{\binom{2n}{k}}

for some nonzero
v_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{\binom{2n}{k}}
. Then
\spn(\phi_{k}(G)v)=\mathbb{R}^{\binom{2n}{k}}
for all nonzero
v\in\mathbb{R}^{\binom{2n}{k}}
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we can consider
v_{0}
an arbitrary element of the standard basis. From
Remark

0.7
, we have
\phi_{k}(G)\subseteq\mathrm{Sym}\mathopen{}\left[2,\binom{2n}{k}\right]\mathclose{}
; therefore,
\spn(\phi_{k}(G)v_{0})=\mathbb{R}^{\binom{2n}{k}}
if and only if the orbit is the entire basis (modulo signs):

\phi_{k}(G)v_{0}=\{e_{\bm{\mu}}\text{ and/or }{-}e_{\bm{\mu}}\mid\bm{\mu}\in\mathcal{C}_{2n,k}\}.

(227)

Now consider some other basis vector
w_{0}\neq v_{0}
. Since there exists some
g\in G
such that
\phi_{k}(g)v_{0}=\pm w_{0}
, it follows that

\begin{split}\phi_{k}(G)v_{0}&=\phi_{k}(G)[\phi_{k}(g^{-1})(\pm w_{0})]\\
&=\phi_{k}(Gg^{-1})(\pm w_{0})=\phi_{k}(G)(\pm w_{0}).\end{split}

(228)

Since the sign is irrelevant when taking the span, we see that the orbit of any basis vector spans the space. Hence the orbit of every nonzero vector does as well.
∎

This result allows us to ignore the second part of
Proposition

0.11
, so that we only have to consider the irreducibility of
\phi_{2k}\big|_{G}
. Furthermore, we do not have to worry about choosing some particular
\bm{\sigma}\in\mathcal{D}_{2n,2k}
to generate our cumulative frame; although the specific form of these tuples changes depending on the choice of fermion-to-qubit mapping, the above results tell us that the behavior of such tight frames is uniform across all of
\mathcal{C}_{2n,2k}
.

Enumerating all possible subgroups to search for the one which gives the largest frame bound (hence smallest shadow norm) is a highly impractical task. Fortunately, this is not necessary, as it turns out that
all
irreps
\phi_{2k}\big|_{G}
yield the same frame bound. To see this, we introduce an alternative, equivalent formulation of frames based on the
frame operator

T\colon V\to V
,

T\coloneqq\sum_{j}\langle x_{j},\cdot\,\rangle x_{j},

(229)

where
\{x_{j}\}_{j}
is a frame for
V
.

Proposition 0.13

(
[
cotfas2010finite
, Theorem 3]
)
.

Let
H
be a finite group,
V
a Hilbert space, and
\varphi\colon H\to\mathrm{U}(V)
an irreducible unitary representation. For any nonzero
v\in V
, the frame operator for the orbit
\varphi(H)v
is

T=\frac{|\varphi(H)v|}{\dim V}\|v\|_{V}^{2}\,\mathbb{I}_{V},

(230)

where
\mathbb{I}_{V}
is the identity operator on
V
.

In general,
T=\alpha\mathbb{I}_{V}
if and only if the frame is tight (with frame bound
\alpha
). In the context of tight frames generated by irreps, this result is essentially a variant on Schur’s lemma. The utility of
Proposition

0.13
in particular is that the frame bound is given explicitly. Our claim that all irreducible subgroups yield the same shadow norm then follows.

Theorem 0.14
.

Let
G\subseteq\mathrm{Sym}^{+}(2,2n)
be irreducible with respect to
\phi_{2k}
. Then

\begin{split}\E_{Q\sim G}\mathopen{}\left[\sum_{\bm{\sigma}\in\mathcal{D}_{2n,2k}}|{\det[Q_{\bm{\sigma},\bm{\tau}}]}|\right]\mathclose{}&\equiv\frac{1}{|G|}\sum_{Q\in G}\sum_{\bm{\sigma}\in\mathcal{D}_{2n,2k}}|[\phi_{2k}(Q)]_{\bm{\sigma}\bm{\tau}}|^{2}\\
&=\mathopen{}\left.\binom{n}{k}\middle/\binom{2n}{2k}\right.\mathclose{}\end{split}

(231)

for all
\bm{\tau}\in\mathcal{C}_{2n,2k}
.

Proof.

Consider the orbit
\phi_{2k}(G)(e_{\bm{\sigma}}|G|^{-1/2})
. Let
\mathrm{Stab}_{G}(e_{\bm{\sigma}})\coloneqq\{Q\in G\mid\phi_{2k}(Q)e_{\bm{\sigma}}=e_{\bm{\sigma}}\}
be its stabilizer subgroup. By the orbit–stabilizer theorem and Lagrange’s theorem
[
grillet2007abstract
]
,

|\phi_{2k}(G)(e_{\bm{\sigma}}|G|^{-1/2})|=\frac{|G|}{|\mathrm{Stab}_{G}(e_{\bm{\sigma}})|}.

(232)

Using
Proposition

0.13
and recalling that the Hilbert space
V
of our frame is
\mathbb{R}^{\binom{2n}{2k}}
, we see that the corresponding frame bound is

\begin{split}\alpha_{\bm{\sigma}}=\|T_{\bm{\sigma}}\|&=\frac{|G|}{|\mathrm{Stab}_{G}(e_{\bm{\sigma}})|}\binom{2n}{2k}^{-1}\mathopen{}\left\|\frac{e_{\bm{\sigma}}}{|G|^{1/2}}\right\|_{2}^{2}\mathclose{}\\
&=\frac{1}{|\mathrm{Stab}_{G}(e_{\bm{\sigma}})|}\binom{2n}{2k}^{-1}.\end{split}

(233)

One must be careful when handling this
|\mathrm{Stab}_{G}(e_{\bm{\sigma}})|
term, since, when constructing the frame operator, we sum over all elements of the orbit, rather than of the group. However, the particular sum that we are interested in, namely
Eq.

231
,
is
over the entire group, and thus precisely double counts the elements of the stabilizer subgroup:

\begin{split}\frac{1}{|G|}\sum_{Q\in G}|[\phi_{2k}(Q)]_{\bm{\sigma}\bm{\tau}}|^{2}&=\sum_{Q\in G}|\langle\phi_{2k}(Q)^{\mathsf{T}}e_{\bm{\sigma}}|G|^{-1/2},e_{\bm{\tau}}\rangle|^{2}\\
&=|\mathrm{Stab}_{G}(e_{\bm{\sigma}})|\sum_{w\in\phi_{2k}(G)(e_{\bm{\sigma}}|G|^{-1/2})}|\langle w,e_{\bm{\tau}}\rangle|^{2}\\
&=|\mathrm{Stab}_{G}(e_{\bm{\sigma}})|\alpha_{\bm{\sigma}}=\binom{2n}{2k}^{-1}.\end{split}

(234)

Since this quantity does not depend on
\bm{\sigma}
, the remaining sum over
\mathcal{D}_{2n,2k}
simply incurs a factor of
|\mathcal{D}_{2n,2k}|=\binom{n}{k}
.
∎

Corollary 0.15
.

In conjunction with
Lemma

0.9
, it immediately follows that

\|\Gamma_{\bm{\tau}}\|_{G}^{2}=\mathopen{}\left.\binom{2n}{2k}\middle/\binom{n}{k}\right.\mathclose{}

(235)

for all irreducible
G\subseteq\mathrm{Sym}^{+}(2,2n)
.

Finally, in order to obtain a concrete example of such tight frames, we show that
\mathrm{Sym}^{+}(2,2n)
is irreducible with respect to
\phi_{k}
for all
1\leq k\leq 2n
. Again, though we are only interested in the even-degree case, we prove the statement for general
k
for completeness. It is then straightforward to show that
\mathrm{Alt}(2n)\subset\mathrm{Sym}^{+}(2,2n)
is also irreducible.

Theorem 0.16
.

Let
G=\mathrm{Sym}^{+}(2,2n)
or
\mathrm{Alt}(2n)
. For all
1\leq k\leq 2n
,
\phi_{k}\big|_{G}
is irreducible.

Proof.

We begin with the case
G=\mathrm{Sym}^{+}(2,2n)
; it will be apparent that the proof methods adapt fully to the
G=\mathrm{Alt}(2n)
case. We show irreducibility via a standard result of character theory
[
fulton2004representation
]
:
\phi_{k}\big|_{G}
is an irrep if and only if

\frac{1}{|G|}\sum_{Q\in G}\tr[\phi_{k}(Q)]^{2}=1.

(236)

Here, the trace of our representation is simply

\tr[\phi_{k}(Q)]=\sum_{\bm{\mu}\in\mathcal{C}_{2n,k}}\det[Q_{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}}].

(237)

Expanding the square yields

\frac{1}{|G|}\sum_{Q\in G}\tr[\phi_{k}(Q)]^{2}=\frac{1}{|G|}\sum_{Q\in G}\mathopen{}\left(\sum_{\bm{\mu}\in\mathcal{C}_{2n,k}}\det[Q_{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}}]^{2}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{\mu},\bm{\nu}\in\mathcal{C}_{2n,k}\\
\bm{\mu}\neq\bm{\nu}\end{subarray}}\det[Q_{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}}]\det[Q_{\bm{\nu},\bm{\nu}}]\right)\mathclose{}.

(238)

We will calculate the average of the each term separately.

Since
|{\det[Q_{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}}]}|\in\{0,1\}
, the diagonal sum is

\frac{1}{|G|}\sum_{Q\in G}\sum_{\bm{\mu}\in\mathcal{C}_{2n,k}}\det[Q_{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}}]^{2}=\sum_{\bm{\mu}\in\mathcal{C}_{2n,k}}\E_{Q\sim G}|{\det[Q_{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}}]}|.

(239)

Intuitively,
\E_{Q\sim G}|{\det[Q_{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}}]}|
is the “density” of the signed permutation matrices whose
(\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu})
submatrix has nonzero subdeterminant. To compute this average, we proceed by a visual argument using the structure of the matrix. Recall that
\det[Q_{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}}]\neq 0
if and only if
Q_{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}}\in\mathrm{Sym}(2,k)
. When writing down a matrix as an array, we can choose any ordering of the row and column indices, so long as this choice is consistent. Therefore, we shall order the indices such that
\bm{\mu}
lies in the first
k
rows/columns:

Q=\mathopen{}\left(\begin{array}[]{c | c}Q_{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}}&*\\
\hline\cr*&*\end{array}\right)\mathclose{}.

(240)

Requiring that
Q_{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}}\in\mathrm{Sym}(2,k)
immediately sets the off-diagonal blocks to be all zeros, hence such a
Q
is block diagonal in this ordering of rows and columns. Furthermore, since
\det Q=1
, the remaining
(2n-k)\times(2n-k)
block must have the same determinant as the
Q_{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}}
block. In other words, a
Q
which satisfies
\det[Q_{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}}]\neq 0
must take the form

Q\in\mathopen{}\left(\begin{array}[]{c | c}\mathrm{Sym}^{+}(2,k)&0\\
\hline\cr 0&\mathrm{Sym}^{+}(2,2n-k)\end{array}\right)\mathclose{}\cup\mathopen{}\left(\begin{array}[]{c | c}\mathrm{Sym}^{-}(2,k)&0\\
\hline\cr 0&\mathrm{Sym}^{-}(2,2n-k)\end{array}\right)\mathclose{},

(241)

where
\mathrm{Sym}^{-}(2,d)\coloneqq\{R\in\mathrm{Sym}(2,d)\mid\det R=-1\}
. Although
\mathrm{Sym}^{-}(2,d)
is not a group, it has the same number of elements as
\mathrm{Sym}^{+}(2,d)
:
|\mathrm{Sym}^{-}(2,d)|=|\mathrm{Sym}^{+}(2,d)|=2^{d}d!/2
. The density is thus

\begin{split}\E_{Q\sim G}|{\det[Q_{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}}]}|&=\frac{|\mathrm{Sym}^{+}(2,k)\oplus\mathrm{Sym}^{+}(2,2n-k)|+|\mathrm{Sym}^{-}(2,k)\oplus\mathrm{Sym}^{-}(2,2n-k)|}{|\mathrm{Sym}^{+}(2,2n)|}\\
&=\frac{2\mathopen{}\left(2^{k}k!\,2^{2n-k}(2n-k)!\right)\mathclose{}/4}{2^{2n}(2n)!/2}=\binom{2n}{k}^{-1},\end{split}

(242)

and so

\sum_{\bm{\mu}\in\mathcal{C}_{2n,k}}\E_{Q\sim G}|{\det[Q_{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}}]}|=1.

(243)

Next we show that the off-diagonal sum of
Eq.

238
vanishes. The argument follows by generalizing the above calculation. For
\bm{\mu}\neq\bm{\nu}
, the tuples (thought of as sets) may overlap
0\leq j\leq k-1
times. We order the matrix representation such that
\bm{\mu}
makes up the first
k
rows/columns as before, but additionally the overlapping indices
\bm{\mu}\cap\bm{\nu}
are placed at the last
j
spots of this
k\times k
block. We then place the remaining part of
\bm{\nu}
in the following
(k-j)\times(k-j)
block. Visually, we have

Q=\mathopen{}\left(\begin{array}[]{c | c | c | c}Q_{\bm{\mu}\setminus\bm{\nu},\bm{\mu}\setminus\bm{\nu}}&0&0&0\\
\hline\cr 0&Q_{\bm{\mu}\cap\bm{\nu},\bm{\mu}\cap\bm{\nu}}&0&0\\
\hline\cr 0&0&Q_{\bm{\nu}\setminus\bm{\mu},\bm{\nu}\setminus\bm{\mu}}&0\\
\hline\cr 0&0&0&*\end{array}\right)\mathclose{},

(244)

where the linear sizes of the four blocks are
k-j
,
j
,
k-j
, and
2(n-k)+j
, respectively. If either
\det[Q_{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}}]
or
\det[Q_{\bm{\nu},\bm{\nu}}]
are
0
, then that term in the sum trivially vanishes. Thus consider the case in which they are both nonzero. Again, since we have the constraint
\det Q=1
, the product of the determinants of all four blocks must be
1
. There are eight cases in which
\det[Q_{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}}]\det[Q_{\bm{\nu},\bm{\nu}}]\neq 0
:
Q\in\mathrm{Sym}^{a}(2,k-j)\oplus\mathrm{Sym}^{b}(2,j)\oplus\mathrm{Sym}^{c}(2,k-j)\oplus\mathrm{Sym}^{d}(2,2(n-k)+j)
, where

(a,b,c,d)\in\mathopen{}\left\{\begin{array}[]{c c c c}({+},{+},{+},{+}),&({+},{+},{-},{-}),&({+},{-},{+},{-}),&({+},{-},{-},{+}),\\
({-},{+},{+},{-}),&({-},{+},{-},{+}),&({-},{-},{+},{+}),&({-},{-},{-},{-})\end{array}\right\}\mathclose{}.

(245)

With this formalism, we can read off the terms in the off-diagonal sum as
\det[Q_{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}}]\det[Q_{\bm{\nu},\bm{\nu}}]=(ab)(bc)=ac
(where our notation means
a=\pm\equiv\pm 1
, etc.). By examining
Eq.

245
, we see that four of the possibilities give
ac=+1
, while the other four give
ac=-1
. Since the number of elements in each of the eight subsets are all the same, exactly half the terms in the sum will cancel with the other half, hence

\sum_{Q\in G}\det[Q_{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}}]\det[Q_{\bm{\nu},\bm{\nu}}]=0\quad\forall\bm{\mu}\neq\bm{\nu}

(246)

as desired.

Thus,

\frac{1}{|G|}\sum_{Q\in G}\tr[\phi_{k}(Q)]^{2}=1,

(247)

and so
\phi_{k}\big|_{G}
is irreducible.

The proof readily adapts for
G=\mathrm{Alt}(2n)
. As we saw in
Eq.

242
, the fact that the matrix elements have signs is irrelevant, as the factors arising due to the wreath product (i.e.,
2^{k}
,
2^{2n-k}
, and
2^{2n}
) exactly cancel out. One may then simply replace every appearance of
\mathrm{Sym}^{+}(2,d)
with
\mathrm{Sym}^{+}(d)\equiv\mathrm{Alt}(d)
and
\mathrm{Sym}^{-}(2,d)
with
\mathrm{Sym}^{-}(d)
without consequence.
∎

In conjunction with the rigorous guarantees of classical shadows,
Theorem

0.3
of the main text follows. To simplify the shadow norm expression, we use Stirling’s approximation, yielding

\left.\binom{2n}{2k}\middle/\binom{n}{k}\right.\approx\binom{n}{k}\sqrt{\pi k}.

(248)

Additionally, the
\log L
factor from using classical shadows (
L
being the number of Majorana operators here) is

\log\mathopen{}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{k}|\mathcal{C}_{2n,2j}|\right)\mathclose{}=\mathcal{O}(k\log n).

(249)

The classical shadow estimator

Here we provide a derivation for the formal expressions of the linear-inversion estimator used in the classical shadows methodology. Recalling
Eq.

206
, and using the linearity of
\mathcal{M}^{-1}_{\mathrm{FGU}}
, the classical shadow is simply

\begin{split}\hat{\rho}_{Q,z}&\equiv\mathcal{M}^{-1}_{\mathrm{FGU}}\mathopen{}\left(U(Q)^{\dagger}|z\rangle\!\langle z|U(Q)\right)\mathclose{}\\
&=\frac{1}{2^{n}}\mathopen{}\left(\mathbb{I}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\lambda_{n,j}^{-1}\sum_{\bm{\mu}\in\mathcal{D}_{2n,2j}}\langle z|\Gamma_{\bm{\mu}}|z\rangle\sum_{\bm{\nu}\in\mathcal{C}_{2n,2j}}\det[Q_{\bm{\mu},\bm{\nu}}]\Gamma_{\bm{\nu}}\right)\mathclose{},\end{split}

(250)

where
\lambda_{n,j}^{-1}=\binom{2n}{2j}/\binom{n}{j}
. Passing this expression into the expectation value estimator, we then obtain

\begin{split}\tr(\Gamma_{\bm{\tau}}\hat{\rho}_{Q,z})&=\frac{1}{2^{n}}\mathopen{}\left(\tr(\Gamma_{\bm{\tau}})+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\lambda_{n,j}^{-1}\sum_{\bm{\mu}\in\mathcal{D}_{2n,2j}}\langle z|\Gamma_{\bm{\mu}}|z\rangle\sum_{\bm{\nu}\in\mathcal{C}_{2n,2j}}\det[Q_{\bm{\mu},\bm{\nu}}]\tr(\Gamma_{\bm{\tau}}\Gamma_{\bm{\nu}})\right)\mathclose{}\\
&=\lambda_{n,k}^{-1}\sum_{\bm{\mu}\in\mathcal{D}_{2n,2k}}\langle z|\Gamma_{\bm{\mu}}|z\rangle\det[Q_{\bm{\mu},\bm{\tau}}]\end{split}

(251)

for all
\bm{\tau}\in\mathcal{C}_{2n,2k}
.

Variance bounds for arbitrary observables

For completeness, we provide a bound on the shadow norm (and hence the estimator variance) of an arbitrary fermionic observable. This result is particularly useful in the context of Hamiltonian averaging (e.g.,
Hamiltonian averaging
).

Any element of
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{even}}^{(n)}
can be written as

O=h_{\varnothing}\mathbb{I}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sum_{\bm{\mu}\in\mathcal{C}_{2n,2j}}h_{\bm{\mu}}\Gamma_{\bm{\mu}},

(252)

where
h_{\bm{\mu}}\in\mathbb{R}
. Without loss of generality, we shall take
\tr O=0
, since the identity component is irrelevant for variance calculations. Furthermore, to simplify the following exposition we shall suppose that
O
is at most a
k
-body operator, so that
h_{\bm{\mu}}=0
for all
|\bm{\mu}|>2k
:

O=\sum_{j=1}^{k}\sum_{\bm{\mu}\in\mathcal{C}_{2n,2j}}h_{\bm{\mu}}\Gamma_{\bm{\mu}}.

(253)

While we do not impose any restriction on
k
, it is worth noting that most physical observables of interest obey
k\leq 4
, with
k=2
being particularly important (for instance, in describing electron–electron interactions).

Because the shadow norm is indeed a norm, it obeys the triangle inequality
[
huang2020predicting
]
. This property allows us to place a bound on

\begin{split}\|O\|_{\mathrm{FGU}}&=\mathopen{}\left\|\sum_{j=1}^{k}\sum_{\bm{\mu}\in\mathcal{C}_{2n,2j}}h_{\bm{\mu}}\Gamma_{\bm{\mu}}\right\|_{\mathrm{FGU}}\mathclose{}\\
&\leq\sum_{j=1}^{k}\sum_{\bm{\mu}\in\mathcal{C}_{2n,2j}}|h_{\bm{\mu}}|\|\Gamma_{\bm{\mu}}\|_{\mathrm{FGU}}\\
&=\sum_{j=1}^{k}\lambda_{n,j}^{-1/2}\sum_{\bm{\mu}\in\mathcal{C}_{2n,2j}}|h_{\bm{\mu}}|.\end{split}

(254)

We therefore obtain an upper bound on the variance of our classical shadow estimator for
\tr(O\rho)
as

\begin{split}\V_{Q,z}\mathopen{}\left[\tr(O\hat{\rho}_{Q,z})\right]\mathclose{}&\leq\|O\|_{\mathrm{FGU}}^{2}-\tr(O\rho)^{2}\\
&\leq\mathopen{}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{k}\lambda_{n,j}^{-1/2}\sum_{\bm{\mu}\in\mathcal{C}_{2n,2j}}|h_{\bm{\mu}}|\right)^{2}\mathclose{}-\tr(O\rho)^{2}.\end{split}

(255)

To get a sense for the asymptotic scaling of this expression, one may further loosen the estimate to obtain

\V_{Q,z}\mathopen{}\left[\tr(O\hat{\rho}_{Q,z})\right]\mathclose{}\leq\mathopen{}\left(\max_{1\leq\ell\leq k}\lambda_{n,\ell}^{-1}\right)\mathclose{}\mathopen{}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{k}\sum_{\bm{\mu}\in\mathcal{C}_{2n,2j}}|h_{\bm{\mu}}|\right)^{2}\mathclose{},

(256)

where
\max_{1\leq\ell\leq k}\lambda_{n,\ell}^{-1}=\mathcal{O}(n^{k})
when
k=\mathcal{O}(1)
.

In follow-up works to this work, exact expressions for the variance and tighter bounds were derived by Wan
et al.

[
wan2023matchgate
]
and O’Gorman
[
ogorman2022fermionic
]
.

Performance guarantees without median-of-means estimation

As remarked in the main text, we do not require the median-of-means technique proposed in the original work
[
huang2020predicting
]
to obtain the same rigorous sampling bounds. Instead, one may simply use the typical sample mean: given
M
independently obtained classical shadows
\hat{\rho}_{1},\ldots,\hat{\rho}_{M}
, define

\omega_{j}(M)\coloneqq\frac{1}{M}\sum_{i=1}^{M}\tr(O_{j}\hat{\rho}_{i})

(257)

for each
j\in\{1,\ldots,L\}
. Below, we state a general condition for which this estimator yields sample complexity equivalent to that of the median-of-means estimator.

Theorem 0.17
.

Suppose the classical shadow estimators
\hat{\rho}_{U,z}
satisfy

-\|O_{j}\|_{\mathcal{U}}^{2}\leq\tr(O_{j}\hat{\rho}_{U,z})\leq\|O_{j}\|_{\mathcal{U}}^{2}

(258)

for all
U\in\mathcal{U}
,
z\in\{0,1\}^{n}
, and
j\in\{1,\ldots,L\}
. Let
\varepsilon,\delta\in(0,1)
. Then by setting

M=\mathopen{}\left(1+\frac{\varepsilon}{3}\right)\mathclose{}\frac{2\log(2L/\delta)}{\varepsilon^{2}}\max_{1\leq j\leq L}\|O_{j}\|_{\mathcal{U}}^{2},

(259)

we ensure that all sample-mean estimators
\omega_{1}(M),\ldots,\omega_{L}(M)
satisfy

|\omega_{j}(M)-\tr(O_{j}\rho)|\leq\varepsilon,

(260)

with probability at least
1-\delta
.

Proof.

The claim follows straightforwardly from Bernstein’s inequality
[
boucheron2013concentration
, Eq. (2.10)]
: for a collection of independent random variables
X_{1},\ldots,X_{M}
satisfying
|X_{i}|\leq b
for all
i\in\{1,\ldots,M\}
, the probability that their empirical mean
\bar{X}\coloneqq\frac{1}{M}\sum_{i=1}^{M}X_{i}
deviates from the true mean
\E[\bar{X}]
by more than
\varepsilon
is bounded as

\Pr\mathopen{}\left[|\bar{X}-\E[\bar{X}]|\geq\varepsilon\right]\mathclose{}\leq 2\exp\mathopen{}\left(-\frac{M^{2}\varepsilon^{2}/2}{v+bM\varepsilon/3}\right)\mathclose{},

(261)

where
v\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{M}\E[X_{i}^{2}]
.

In our setting, for each
j\in\{1,\ldots,L\}
, we have
\bar{X}=\omega_{j}(M)
,
b=\|O_{j}\|_{\mathcal{U}}^{2}
, and
v=M\|O_{j}\|_{\mathcal{U}}^{2}
(recall that the shadow norm squared is precisely
\E[X_{i}^{2}]

[
huang2020predicting
]
). The concentration inequality then reads

\Pr\mathopen{}\left[|\omega_{j}(M)-\tr(O_{j}\rho)|\geq\varepsilon\right]\mathclose{}\leq 2\exp\mathopen{}\left[-\frac{M\varepsilon^{2}/2}{\|O_{j}\|_{\mathcal{U}}^{2}(1+\varepsilon/3)}\right]\mathclose{}.

(262)

If we require that each probability of failure be no more than
\delta/L
, then from a union bound over all
L
events, we can succeed with probability at least
1-\delta
by setting

2\exp\mathopen{}\left(-\frac{M\varepsilon^{2}/2}{\max_{1\leq j\leq L}\|O_{j}\|_{\mathcal{U}}^{2}(1+\varepsilon/3)}\right)\mathclose{}=\frac{\delta}{L}.

(263)

Solving for
M
yields
Eq.

259
.
∎

For practical purposes, one typically desires that
\varepsilon
be small, thus
(1+\varepsilon/3)\approx 1
. Importantly,
Theorem

0.17
guarantees optimal scaling with the failure probability
\delta
and the number
L
of observables, using the sample mean rather than median-of-means estimation. The key detail which enables this observation is the boundedness of the classical shadows estimators,
Eq.

258
. This condition holds for the ensembles presented in this work,
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{FGU}}
and
\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{NC}}
(see
Computations with the number-conserving modification
), when taking the observables
O_{j}
as Majorana operators. It is also satisfied for estimating Pauli observables using the
\mathrm{Cl}(1)^{\otimes n}
ensemble of the original work
[
huang2020predicting
]
, indicating that median-of-means is redundant for estimating qubit RDMs as well.

Interestingly,
Eq.

259
features significantly smaller numerical factors than what is obtained from the median-of-means approach, although we recognize that the proof techniques presented in Ref.
[
huang2020predicting
]
were not particularly optimized in this regard.

Information-theoretic lower bounds on predicting local fermionic observables

Our main result,
Theorem

0.3
, establishes that, with high probability,

M=\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\left(\frac{\lambda_{n,k}^{-1}\log L}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right)\mathclose{},\quad\lambda_{n,k}^{-1}=\mathopen{}\left.\binom{2n}{2k}\middle/\binom{n}{k}\right.\mathclose{}=\mathcal{O}(n^{k}),

(264)

samples from our fermionic classical shadows protocol suffice to estimate all
L=\mathcal{O}(n^{2k})

k
-body Majorana operators to accuracy
\varepsilon
. In this section, we prove a matching lower bound showing that, asymptotically, that many samples are also
necessary
for any prediction algorithm to accomplish this task, given only the ability to perform local fermionic measurements. The claim is based on information-theoretic arguments
[
flammia2012quantum
,
haah2017sample
]
, which follows by adapting an analogous result of Huang
et al.

[
huang2020predicting
]
about local qubit observables to the fermion setting. We refer the reader to their paper for a lucid description of the intuition behind such arguments. We clarify that such lower bounds (including the one that we establish here) assumes only single-copy measurements, and does not apply to the task of predicting observables that are specified in advance. In other words, our result only applies to protocols which are tasked to learn all (local fermionic) observables equally well.

To state the formal theorem, we first need a general definition for a local fermionic measurement.

Definition 0.18
.

Let
\{|\varphi_{i}\rangle\}_{i}
be a collection of pure fermionic Gaussian states and
\{w_{i}\}_{i}\subset\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
some weights. We say that
E=\{w_{i}2^{n}|\varphi_{i}\rangle\!\langle\varphi_{i}|\}_{i}
is a
local fermionic measurement
if it is a POVM, i.e., if
2^{n}\sum_{i}w_{i}|\varphi_{i}\rangle\!\langle\varphi_{i}|=\mathbb{I}
.

This definition encompasses any type of measurement in a (potentially overcomplete) basis of free-fermion states. Physically, this corresponds to any POVM that can be implemented with strictly noninteracting fermionic operations. A prime example is our
\mathrm{FGU}(n)
ensemble. Furthermore, because we do not place any restrictions on the parity of the Gaussian states
|\varphi_{i}\rangle
, it also applies to the generalized matchgate ensembles of
[
wan2023matchgate
]
, which are extensions of our
\mathrm{FGU}(n)
to all of
\mathrm{O}(2n)
[as well as its corresponding Clifford intersection,
\mathrm{Sym}(2,2n)
] that retain the same performance guarantees for local-observable prediction.

We can now state the lower bound for learning from local fermionic measurements. We phrase the theorem parallel to the language of
[
huang2020predicting
, Theorem S6]
, to underscore the similarity in transferring the concept to the fermionic setting.

Theorem 0.19

(Formal version of
Theorem

0.4
)
.

Fix a sequence of local fermionic measurements
E_{1},\ldots,E_{M}
on an
n
-mode system. Suppose that, given a collection of
L
observables
-\mathbb{I}\preceq O_{1},\ldots,O_{L}\preceq\mathbb{I}
, with Majorana degree exactly
2k
, there exists a machine (with arbitrary runtime, as long as it always terminates) that can use the outcomes of
E_{1},\ldots,E_{M}
on
M
copies of an unknown quantum state
\rho
to predict
\tr(O_{1}\rho),\ldots,\tr(O_{L}\rho)
, each up to additive error
\varepsilon
, with high probability. Assuming
L\leq\binom{2n}{2k}
, then necessarily

M\geq\Omega\mathopen{}\left(\frac{\lambda_{n,k}^{-1}\log L}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right)\mathclose{},

(265)

where
\lambda_{n,k}^{-1}=\binom{2n}{2k}/\binom{n}{k}
.

The idea behind the proof of Huang
et al.

[
huang2020predicting
]
is a quantum communication protocol between two parties, Alice and Bob. They have agreed on a codebook of quantum states
\{\rho_{1},\ldots,\rho_{L}\}
that encodes classical information in the following way: there exists a collection of observables
O_{1},\ldots,O_{L}
such that learning the
i
th property uniquely identifies the
i
th state. Suppose that this procedure can tolerate error at most
\varepsilon
in the learned property to correctly identify the sent state. Then, Alice can use the codebook to pass some message
i
to Bob by sending him
M
copies of
\rho_{i}
. By performing the appropriate single-copy measurements, Bob can learn
\tr(O_{j}\rho_{i})
for each
j\in\{1,\ldots,L\}
with sufficient accuracy, thereby identifying which state Alice sent and decoding her message.

However, this protocol does not fully embody the task we are interested in. Namely, Bob (the learner) does not know in advance which observables
O_{j}
correspond to which states
\rho_{i}
, and only after measuring all the copies is that knowledge revealed to him. The communication protocol can be modified to reflect this aspect by introducing an interfering agent Loki, who intercepts each state sent by Alice and applies a random unitary transformation
\rho_{i}\mapsto U\rho_{i}U^{\dagger}
before sending it along to Bob. Note that Loki applies the same
U
to all
M
copies. If Bob knew which
U
Loki performed, then he could simply learn
\{UO_{j}U^{\dagger}\}_{j=1}^{L}
instead. However, without that information, the task is more challenging—Bob needs to devise a strategy which succeeds for all possible
U
from Loki’s distribution. Only after Bob measures all
M
copies does Loki eventually reveal the
U
that he performed (as otherwise Bob would be hopelessly lost). The question is then to determine the minimum number of copies that Alice must send in order for Bob to decode her message in this scenario (with high probability).

Because the task we are interested in only considers learning
k
-local fermionic properties, we make the following restrictions to this communication protocol. First, the random unitary that Loki chooses is restricted to be Gaussian, because otherwise he could transform a local observable into a nonlocal one, and Bob’s prediction machine has no guarantee to work for nonlocal properties. Second, because the
2k
-degree Majorana operators
\Gamma_{\bm{\mu}}
,
\bm{\mu}\in\mathcal{C}_{2n,2k}
, form a complete basis for the space of all
2k
-degree operators, without loss of generality we can take them to be the observables
O_{j}
. This requires us to assume
L\leq|\mathcal{C}_{2n,2k}|=\binom{2n}{2k}
, so that the codebook uniquely encodes each message. To lighten notation, we will simply write
\Gamma_{\bm{\mu}}\equiv\Gamma_{i}
for some ordering of
\bm{\mu}
into
i=1,\ldots,L
.

With the problem now set up, we can prove
Theorem

0.19
using this modified quantum communication protocol.

Proof (of
Theorem

0.19
).

Define the codebook via

\rho_{i}\coloneqq\frac{1}{2^{n}}\mathopen{}\left(\mathbb{I}+\varepsilon\Gamma_{i}\right)\mathclose{}.

(266)

This construction guarantees that the states are uniquely identifiable, but
\varepsilon
-hard to do so:

\tr(\Gamma_{j}\rho_{i})=\varepsilon\delta_{ij}.

(267)

The protocol then proceeds as follows. Alice randomly selects an integer
X\sim\{1,\ldots,L\}
, prepares
M
copies of
\rho_{X}
, and sends them to Bob. In between, Loki intercepts the states, samples a random
Q\sim\mathrm{O}(2n)
, and applies the corresponding fermionic Gaussian unitary, mapping
\rho_{X}\mapsto U(Q)\rho_{X}U(Q)^{\dagger}
. Bob then performs his fixed sequence of local fermionic measurements
E_{j}
,
j=1,\ldots,M
, to each copy of
U(Q)\rho_{X}U(Q)^{\dagger}
. Denote his measurement outcomes as
Y=(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{M})
.

Loki then reveals the randomly drawn
Q
to Bob. Because
U(Q)
preserves fermionic locality, the observables
U(Q)\Gamma_{j}U(Q)^{\dagger}
remain
2k
-degree. Thus Bob’s machine can predict expectation values of
U(Q)\Gamma_{j}U(Q)^{\dagger}
from
Y
, and so in principle he can recover the original properties, since
\tr(\Gamma_{j}\rho_{X})=\tr[(U(Q)\Gamma_{j}U(Q)^{\dagger})(U(Q)\rho_{X}U(Q)^{\dagger})]
. His decryption
\overline{X}
of Alice’s message
X
is simply the choice of
\Gamma_{\overline{X}}
with the largest estimated value for
\tr(\Gamma_{\overline{X}}\rho_{X})
returned by his prediction machine.

To determine how many copies of
\rho_{X}
that Bob needs for this procedure, one can examine the information content of the measurement outcomes
Y
. This analysis was already performed in
[
huang2020predicting
]
with fairly high generality, so we simply quote the pertinent results here [adapted from their Eqs. (S108), (S109), and (S112)]:

\displaystyle\Omega(\log L)\leq I(X:Y|Q)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=1}^{M}I(X:E_{j}\text{ on }U(Q)\rho_{X}U(Q)^{\dagger}),

(268)

\displaystyle I(X:E_{j}\text{ on }U(Q)\rho_{X}U(Q)^{\dagger})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i}\frac{\E_{X,Q}[p_{j,i}^{2}]-\E_{X,Q}[p_{j,i}]^{2}}{\E_{X,Q}[p_{j,i}]},

(269)

where
I
is the (conditional) mutual information and

p_{j,i}\coloneqq w_{j,i}2^{n}\langle\varphi_{j,i}|U(Q)\rho_{X}U(Q)^{\dagger}|\varphi_{j,i}\rangle

(270)

are the probabilities associated with the local fermionic POVMs
E_{j}=\{w_{j,i}2^{n}|\varphi_{j,i}\rangle\!\langle\varphi_{j,i}|\}_{i}
. Intuitively,
Eq.

268
establishes a connection between the amount of information that Alice can encode (
\log L
bits, using a codebook of length
L
) and the maximum amount of information that the measurements
E_{j}
on the disrupted state
U(Q)\rho_{X}U(Q)^{\dagger}
can reveal about her message
X
. It does so through
I(X:Y|Q)
, the mutual information between Alice’s message
X
and Bob’s measurement data
Y
, conditioned on Loki’s random unitary
U(Q)
. Meanwhile,
Eq.

269
establishes a bound on the information content of each individual measurement
E_{j}
in terms of their outcome distributions.

Note that
Eq.

269
requires evaluating Haar averages over
Q\sim\mathrm{O}(2n)
with respect to “twirling” by
U(Q)
. Fortunately, recent work by Wan
et al.

[
wan2023matchgate
]
did precisely this,
36
36

36

For comparison, in
Computations with the fermionic Gaussian Clifford ensemble
we had established specific variants of these results, considering subgroups
\mathrm{Alt}(2n)\subset\mathrm{Sym}^{+}(2,2n)\subset\mathrm{SO}(2n)\subset\mathrm{O}(2n)
and averaging only over operators of the form
A_{t}=\sum_{z\in\{0,1\}^{n}}|z\rangle\!\langle z|^{\otimes t}
. See
Group-theoretic Error Mitigation Enabled by Classical Shadows and Symmetries
,
Background on matchgate shadows
for further exposition.
deriving expressions for the following averages:

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{(1)}(A_{1})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\E_{Q\sim\mathrm{O}(2n)}\mathopen{}\left[U(Q)^{\dagger}(A_{1})U(Q)\right]\mathclose{}=\frac{\tr(A_{1})}{2^{n}}\mathbb{I},

(271)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{(2)}(A_{2})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\E_{Q\sim\mathrm{O}(2n)}\mathopen{}\left[(U(Q)^{\otimes 2})^{\dagger}(A_{2})U(Q)^{\otimes 2}\right]\mathclose{}=\frac{1}{4^{n}}\sum_{\ell=0}^{2n}\tr(\Upsilon_{\ell}^{\dagger}A_{2})\Upsilon_{\ell}

(272)

where
A_{t}\in(\mathbb{C}^{2^{n}\times 2^{n}})^{\otimes t}
are arbitrary, and

\Upsilon_{\ell}\coloneqq\frac{(-1)^{\binom{\ell}{2}}}{\sqrt{\binom{2n}{\ell}}}\sum_{\bm{\mu}\in\mathcal{C}_{2n,\ell}}\Gamma_{\bm{\mu}}^{\otimes 2}.

(273)

The average over
p_{j,i}
can be computed using
Eq.

271
:

\begin{split}\E_{X,Q}[p_{j,i}]&=w_{j,i}2^{n}\tr\mathopen{}\left(\E_{X}\mathopen{}\left[\mathcal{E}^{(1)}(|\varphi_{j,i}\rangle\!\langle\varphi_{j,i}|)\rho_{X}\right]\mathclose{}\right)\mathclose{}\\
&=w_{j,i}\tr\mathopen{}\left(\E_{X}\rho_{X}\right)\mathclose{}=w_{j,i}.\end{split}

(274)

Similarly, the average over
p_{j,i}^{2}
can be computed using
Eq.

272
. We will use an additional property of the twirl, namely that it is
\mathrm{O}(2n)
-invariant:

\mathcal{E}^{(t)}\mathopen{}\left(U(R)^{\otimes t}(\cdot)(U(R)^{\otimes t})^{\dagger}\right)\mathclose{}=\mathcal{E}^{(t)}(\cdot)

(275)

for all
t
and any
R\in\mathrm{O}(2n)
. Thus, in conjunction with the fact that every Gaussian state can be written as
|\varphi\rangle=U(R)|0\rangle
for some
R\in\mathrm{O}(2n)
, it follows that

\E_{Q\sim\mathrm{O}(2n)}\mathopen{}\left[(U(Q)^{\otimes t})^{\dagger}(|\varphi\rangle\!\langle\varphi|)^{\otimes t}U(Q)^{\otimes t}\right]\mathclose{}=\E_{Q\sim\mathrm{O}(2n)}\mathopen{}\left[(U(Q)^{\otimes t})^{\dagger}(|0\rangle\!\langle 0|)^{\otimes t}U(Q)^{\otimes t}\right]\mathclose{}

(276)

for all Gaussian states
|\varphi\rangle
. This property greatly simplifies the calculation of
\E_{X,Q}[p_{j,i}^{2}]
:

\begin{split}\E_{X,Q}[p_{j,i}^{2}]&=w_{j,i}^{2}4^{n}\tr\mathopen{}\left(\E_{X}\mathopen{}\left[\mathcal{E}^{(2)}(|\varphi_{j,i}\rangle\!\langle\varphi_{j,i}|^{\otimes 2})\rho_{X}^{\otimes 2}\right]\mathclose{}\right)\mathclose{}\\
&=w_{j,i}^{2}4^{n}\tr\mathopen{}\left(\mathcal{E}^{(2)}(|0\rangle\!\langle 0|^{\otimes 2})\E_{X}[\rho_{X}^{\otimes 2}]\right)\mathclose{}.\end{split}

(277)

Wan
et al.

[
wan2023matchgate
]
have evaluated
\mathcal{E}^{(2)}(|0\rangle\!\langle 0|^{\otimes 2})
as well, finding that

\mathcal{E}^{(2)}(|0\rangle\!\langle 0|^{\otimes 2})=\frac{1}{4^{n}}\sum_{\ell=0}^{n}\frac{\binom{n}{\ell}}{\binom{2n}{2\ell}}\sum_{\bm{\mu}\in\mathcal{C}_{2n,2\ell}}\Gamma_{\bm{\mu}}^{\otimes 2}.

(278)

Tracing this with
\rho_{X}^{\otimes 2}
then yields

\begin{split}\tr\mathopen{}\left(\mathcal{E}^{(2)}(|0\rangle\!\langle 0|^{\otimes 2})\rho_{X}^{\otimes 2}\right)\mathclose{}&=\frac{1}{4^{n}}\sum_{\ell=0}^{n}\frac{\binom{n}{\ell}}{\binom{2n}{2\ell}}\sum_{\bm{\mu}\in\mathcal{C}_{2n,2\ell}}\tr(\Gamma_{\bm{\mu}}\rho_{X})^{2}\\
&=\frac{1}{4^{n}}\mathopen{}\left(1+\frac{\binom{n}{k}}{\binom{2n}{2k}}\varepsilon^{2}\right)\mathclose{},\end{split}

(279)

which implies that

\E_{X,Q}[p_{j,i}^{2}]=w_{j,i}^{2}(1+\lambda_{n,k}\varepsilon^{2}).

(280)

Inserting these results into the mutual information bounds
Eq.

269
gives

\begin{split}I(X:E_{j}\text{ on }U(Q)\rho_{X}U(Q)^{\dagger})&\leq\sum_{i}\frac{\E_{X,Q}[p_{j,i}^{2}]-\E_{X,Q}[p_{j,i}]^{2}}{\E_{X,Q}[p_{j,i}]}\\
&=\sum_{i}\frac{w_{j,i}^{2}(1+\lambda_{n,k}\varepsilon^{2})-w_{j,i}^{2}}{w_{j,i}}\\
&=\lambda_{n,k}\varepsilon^{2}\sum_{i}w_{j,i}=\lambda_{n,k}\varepsilon^{2}.\end{split}

(281)

Finally, we apply the inequality of
Eq.

268
,

\begin{split}\Omega(\log L)&\leq\sum_{j=1}^{M}I(X:E_{j}\text{ on }U(Q)\rho_{X}U(Q)^{\dagger})\\
&\leq M\lambda_{n,k}\varepsilon^{2},\end{split}

(282)

which implies
Eq.

265
as claimed.
∎

Computations with the number-conserving modification

We now prove
Theorem

0.5
of the main text. Many of the techniques used here follow straightforwardly from the fermionic formalism developed in
Computations with the fermionic Gaussian Clifford ensemble
, along with the tools used to study the single-qubit Clifford ensemble in the original work on classical shadows
[
huang2020predicting
]
. In
The shadow norm
, we first evaluate the channel
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{NC}}
[which again is diagonalized by the Majorana operators;
Eq.

287
] and provide an expression for its eigenvalues/shadow norm [
Eq.

289
]. Then, recognizing that generically do not possess a closed-form expression, in
Universal upper bounds on the shadow norm
we obtain an upper bound on the shadow norm for this ensemble, which is asymptotically optimal [
Eq.

304
].

For ease of notation, with
u\in\mathrm{Sym}(n)
, we shall write
U(u)^{\dagger}a_{p}U(u)=a_{u(p)}
, where
u(p)
is understood in the sense of the action of the permutation
u
on the mode indices
\{0,\ldots,n-1\}
. We further generalize this notation to act on
\{0,\ldots,2n-1\}
, in accordance with the definition of Majorana operators. Consider
\bm{\mu}\in\mathcal{C}_{2n,k}
, where each index takes the form
\mu_{j}=2q_{j}+x_{j}
for
q_{j}\in\{0,2,\ldots,2n-4,2n-2\}
and
x_{j}\in\{0,1\}
. We define
\tilde{u}(\bm{\mu})\coloneqq(2u(q_{1})+x_{1},\ldots,2u(q_{k})+x_{k})
, so that
U(u)^{\dagger}\Gamma_{\bm{\mu}}U(u)=\Gamma_{\tilde{u}(\bm{\mu})}
. Note that
\tilde{u}(\bm{\mu})
is not necessarily ordered monotonically, so
\Gamma_{\tilde{u}(\bm{\mu})}
may differ from our standard definition of the Majorana operators by a minus sign. This detail is irrelevant to our present analysis, so we shall ignore it.

The shadow norm

The ensemble we consider here is

\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{NC}}=\{V\circ U(u)\mid V\in\mathrm{Cl}(1)^{\otimes n},\,u\in\mathrm{Alt}(n)\}.

(283)

We wish to evaluate the following expression for the classical shadows channel:

\begin{split}\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{NC}}(\Gamma_{\bm{\mu}})&=\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}V\sim\mathrm{Cl}(1)^{\otimes n}\\
u\sim\mathrm{Alt}(n)\end{subarray}}\mathopen{}\left[\sum_{z\in\{0,1\}^{n}}\langle z|VU(u)\Gamma_{\bm{\mu}}U(u)^{\dagger}V^{\dagger}|z\rangle U(u)^{\dagger}V^{\dagger}|z\rangle\!\langle z|VU(u)\right]\mathclose{}\\
&=\E_{u\sim\mathrm{Alt}(n)}\mathopen{}\left[U(u)^{\dagger}\sum_{z\in\{0,1\}^{n}}\E_{V\sim\mathrm{Cl}(1)^{\otimes n}}\Big[\langle z|V\Gamma_{\tilde{u}^{-1}(\bm{\mu})}V^{\dagger}|z\rangle V^{\dagger}|z\rangle\!\langle z|V\Big]U(u)\right]\mathclose{}.\end{split}

(284)

The average over
\mathrm{Cl}(1)^{\otimes n}
is precisely the same quantity evaluated in Ref.
[
huang2020predicting
]
. For convenience, we restate their results here: let
|x\rangle=|x_{1}\rangle\otimes\cdots\otimes|x_{n}\rangle
be a product state over
n
qubits and
A,B,C
be Hermitian matrices which decompose into the same tensor product structure,
A=A_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes A_{n}
, etc. Then

\E_{V\sim\mathrm{Cl}(1)^{\otimes n}}\mathopen{}\left[V^{\dagger}|x\rangle\!\langle x|V\langle x|VAV^{\dagger}|x\rangle\right]\mathclose{}=\bigotimes_{j=1}^{n}\mathopen{}\left(\frac{A_{j}+\tr(A_{j})I}{6}\right)\mathclose{},

(285)

and for each
j
such that
\tr B_{j}=\tr C_{j}=0
,

\E_{V_{j}\sim\mathrm{Cl}(1)}\mathopen{}\left[V_{j}^{\dagger}|x_{j}\rangle\!\langle x_{j}|V_{j}\langle x_{j}|V_{j}B_{j}V_{j}^{\dagger}|x_{j}\rangle\langle x_{j}|V_{j}C_{j}V_{j}^{\dagger}|x_{j}\rangle\right]\mathclose{}=\frac{B_{j}C_{j}+C_{j}B_{j}+\tr(B_{j}C_{j})I}{24}.

(286)

To understand the tensor product structure of Majorana operators, we must fix some qubit mapping. Understanding
\Gamma_{\tilde{u}^{-1}(\bm{\mu})}
as a Pauli operator under such a mapping, we use
Eq.

285
to obtain

\begin{split}\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{NC}}(\Gamma_{\bm{\mu}})&=\E_{u\sim\mathrm{Alt}(n)}\mathopen{}\left[\frac{1}{3^{\mathrm{loc}(\Gamma_{\tilde{u}^{-1}(\bm{\mu})})}}U(u)^{\dagger}\Gamma_{\tilde{u}^{-1}(\bm{\mu})}U(u)\right]\mathclose{}\\
&=\E_{u\sim\mathrm{Alt}(n)}\mathopen{}\left[\frac{1}{3^{\mathrm{loc}(\Gamma_{\tilde{u}^{-1}(\bm{\mu})})}}\right]\mathclose{}\Gamma_{\bm{\mu}},\end{split}

(287)

where
\mathrm{loc}(\Gamma_{\tilde{u}^{-1}(\bm{\mu})})
is the qubit locality of
\Gamma_{\tilde{u}^{-1}(\bm{\mu})}
. Since
u\mapsto u^{-1}
is a bijection, we may equivalently express the average over
\mathrm{Alt}(n)
using
\tilde{u}(\bm{\mu})
rather than its inverse.

Let
\lambda_{\bm{\mu}}
be the eigenvalues of
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{NC}}
, given above in
Eq.

287
. For the calculation of the shadow norm, we have

\displaystyle\|\Gamma_{\bm{\mu}}\|_{\mathrm{NC}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\max_{\text{states }\rho}\mathopen{}\left(\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}V\sim\mathrm{Cl}(1)^{\otimes n}\\
u\sim\mathrm{Alt}(n)\end{subarray}}\mathopen{}\left[\sum_{z\in\{0,1\}^{n}}\langle z|VU(u)\rho U(u)^{\dagger}V^{\dagger}|z\rangle\langle z|VU(u)\mathcal{M}^{-1}(\Gamma_{\bm{\mu}})U(u)^{\dagger}V^{\dagger}|z\rangle^{2}\right]\mathclose{}\right)\mathclose{},

(288)

\displaystyle=\lambda_{\bm{\mu}}^{-2}\max_{\text{states }\rho}\mathopen{}\left(\E_{u\sim\mathrm{Alt}(n)}\mathopen{}\left[\tr\mathopen{}\left(U(u)^{\dagger}\rho U(u)\sum_{z\in\{0,1\}^{n}}\E_{V\sim\mathrm{Cl}(1)^{\otimes n}}\Big[V^{\dagger}|z\rangle\!\langle z|V\langle z|V\Gamma_{\tilde{u}^{-1}(\bm{\mu})}V^{\dagger}|z\rangle^{2}\Big]\right)\mathclose{}\right]\mathclose{}\right)\mathclose{}.

We then use
Eq.

285
to evaluate the Clifford average over the identity factors of
\Gamma_{\tilde{u}^{-1}(\bm{\mu})}
, and
Eq.

286
for the nontrivial factors:

\begin{split}\|\Gamma_{\bm{\mu}}\|_{\mathrm{NC}}^{2}&=\lambda_{\bm{\mu}}^{-2}\max_{\text{states }\rho}\mathopen{}\left(\E_{u\sim\mathrm{Alt}(n)}\mathopen{}\left[\tr\mathopen{}\left(U(u)^{\dagger}\rho U(u)\frac{1}{3^{\mathrm{loc}(\Gamma_{\tilde{u}^{-1}(\bm{\mu})})}}\right)\mathclose{}\right]\mathclose{}\right)\mathclose{}\\
&=\lambda_{\bm{\mu}}^{-1}.\end{split}

(289)

Universal upper bounds on the shadow norm

Although there is no closed-form expression for
\lambda_{\bm{\mu}}^{-1}
, we can still obtain a nontrivial estimate for it. To do so, we will evaluate the qubit locality with respect to the Jordan–Wigner transformation. This serves as a universal upper bound for all encodings, since the Jordan–Wigner mapping is maximally nonlocal. We formalize this notion with Jensen’s inequality: since
x\mapsto 3^{-x}
is concave, we have that

\E_{u\sim\mathrm{Alt}(n)}\mathopen{}\left[3^{-\mathrm{loc}(\Gamma_{\tilde{u}(\bm{\mu})})}\right]\mathclose{}\geq 3^{-\E_{u\sim\mathrm{Alt}(n)}\mathopen{}\left[\mathrm{loc}(\Gamma_{\tilde{u}(\bm{\mu})})\right]\mathclose{}},

(290)

where
\mathrm{loc}(\,\cdot\,)
is with respect to any arbitrary encoding. Then let
\mathrm{loc}_{\mathrm{JW}}(\,\cdot\,)
be the qubit locality specifically under the Jordan–Wigner transformation. Since it is maximally nonlocal, so is its average locality (over a fixed fermionic degree of
2k
), and hence

\max_{\bm{\mu}\in\mathcal{C}_{2n,2k}}\lambda_{\bm{\mu}}^{-1}\leq\max_{\bm{\mu}\in\mathcal{C}_{2n,2k}}3^{\E_{u\sim\mathrm{Alt}(n)}\mathopen{}\left[\mathrm{loc}(\Gamma_{\tilde{u}(\bm{\mu})})\right]\mathclose{}}\leq\max_{\bm{\mu}\in\mathcal{C}_{2n,2k}}3^{\E_{u\sim\mathrm{Alt}(n)}\mathopen{}\left[\mathrm{loc}_{\mathrm{JW}}(\Gamma_{\tilde{u}(\bm{\mu})})\right]\mathclose{}}.

(291)

Thus for the rest of this section, all notions of qubit locality will be understood with respect to the Jordan–Wigner transformation exclusively.

Since the qubit locality of Majorana operators varies within a given
\mathcal{C}_{2n,2k}
, we obtain an upper bound by considering the most nonlocal
2k
-degree operators. Fortunately, under Jordan–Wigner it is simple to identify such operators. Let
W\in\{X,Y\}
and
\bm{q}\in\mathcal{C}_{n,2k}
; maximum locality is achieved by operators of the form

\displaystyle P_{\bm{q}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\prod_{i=1}^{k}W_{q_{2i-1}}Z_{q_{2i-1}+1}\cdots Z_{q_{2i}-1}W_{q_{2i}},

(292)

\displaystyle\mathrm{loc}(P_{\bm{q}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathopen{}\left(q_{2i}-q_{2i-1}+1\right)\mathclose{}.

(293)

Note that we require
n\geq 2k
for such a Pauli operator to exist. (For the fringe cases in which
n<2k
, since we are evaluating an upper bound, the final result still holds.)

By applying a permutation
u
on
\bm{q}
, one changes this locality by reducing or lengthening the various “Jordan–Wigner strings” of Pauli-
Z
operators in between each pair
(q_{2i-1},q_{2i})
. Observe that
\mathrm{loc}(P_{\bm{q}})\in\{2k,\ldots,n\}
. Therefore, if we can calculate the number of permutations which correspond to each level of locality, we may compute

\E_{u\sim\mathrm{Alt}(n)}\mathopen{}\left[3^{-\mathrm{loc}(P_{u(\bm{q})})}\right]\mathclose{}=\frac{2}{n!}\sum_{\ell=2k}^{n}3^{-\ell}\,|\{u\in\mathrm{Alt}(n)\mid\mathrm{loc}(P_{u(\bm{q})})=\ell\}|.

(294)

We determine the size of this set in three steps. First, for a given configuration
u(\bm{q})
, we count the number of equivalent permutations which give the same Pauli operator, modulo signs. These permutations merely reorder the
2k
relevant indices and the
n-2k
remaining indices independently. Accounting for the fact that the entire permutation must be even parity, we have

\begin{split}|\mathrm{Alt}(2k)\oplus\mathrm{Alt}(n-2k)|+|\mathrm{Sym}^{-}(2k)\oplus\mathrm{Sym}^{-}(n-2k)|&=\frac{(2k)!}{2}\frac{(n-2k)!}{2}+\frac{(2k)!}{2}\frac{(n-2k)!}{2}\\
&=\frac{(2k)!(n-2k)!}{2}\end{split}

(295)

such permutations. With this factor at hand, we now only need to consider unique combinations of indices—that is, we assume
u(q_{1})<\cdots<u(q_{2k})
in the sequel.

Next, we calculate how many configurations of Jordan–Wigner strings give rise to a qubit locality of exactly
\ell
. Let
l_{i}\coloneqq u(q_{2i})-u(q_{2i-1})>0
. This problem is equivalent to finding all
k
-tuples
(l_{1},\ldots,l_{k})
of positive integers such that

\sum_{i=1}^{k}(l_{i}+1)=\ell.

(296)

This is an instance of the classic “stars-and-bars” combinatorial problem, wherein we wish to fit
\ell-k
objects into
k
bins such that each bin has at least
1
object. There are
\binom{\ell-k-1}{k-1}
ways to do so.

Finally, we need to account for the
n-\ell
remaining indices which were not fixed by
Eq.

296
. These indices correspond to the qubits
outside
of the Jordan–Wigner strings, i.e., on which
P_{u(\bm{q})}
acts trivially. By a similar combinatorial argument, there are a total of
k+1
such spaces to place these trivial indices, of which we may select
j\in\{1,\ldots,k+1\}
. Again we use the stars-and-bars argument: there are
n-\ell
objects we wish to place into
j
bins, which can be accomplished in
\binom{n-\ell-1}{j-1}
unique ways. Summing over all possible values of
j
gives us

\begin{split}\sum_{j=1}^{k+1}\binom{k+1}{j}\binom{n-\ell-1}{j-1}&=\sum_{j=1}^{k+1}\binom{k+1}{k+1-j}\binom{n-\ell-1}{j-1}\\
&=\sum_{j=0}^{k}\binom{k+1}{k-j}\binom{n-\ell-1}{j}=\binom{n+k-\ell}{k}\end{split}

(297)

different combinations of the
n-\ell
trivial indices. Note that the Chu–Vandermonde identity was applied to evaluate the sum in the final line.

Reconciling the three steps of our calculation, we obtain

|\{u\in\mathrm{Sym}(n)\mid\mathrm{loc}(P_{u(\bm{q})})=\ell\}|=(2k)!(n-2k)!\binom{\ell-k-1}{k-1}\binom{n+k-\ell}{k},

(298)

and so
Eq.

294
can be expressed as

\begin{split}\E_{u\sim\mathrm{Sym}(n)}\mathopen{}\left[3^{-\mathrm{loc}(P_{u(\bm{q})})}\right]\mathclose{}&=\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\ell=2k}^{n}3^{-\ell}\,|\{u\in\mathrm{Sym}(n)\mid\mathrm{loc}(P_{u(\bm{q})})=\ell\}|\\
&=\frac{(2k)!(n-2k)!}{n!}\sum_{\ell=2k}^{n}3^{-\ell}\binom{\ell-k-1}{k-1}\binom{n+k-\ell}{k}.\end{split}

(299)

To evaluate this sum, first we relabel the index
\ell
to run from
0
to
n-2k
, so that the summand becomes
3^{-(\ell+2k)}\binom{\ell+k-1}{k-1}\binom{n-k-\ell}{k}
. Using the combinatorial identity

\binom{n-k}{\ell}\binom{n-k-\ell}{k}=\binom{n-k}{k}\binom{n-2k}{\ell},

(300)

we have

\begin{split}\binom{n-k-\ell}{k}&=\binom{n-k}{k}\frac{(n-2k)!}{(n-2k-\ell)!\ell!}\frac{(n-k-\ell)!\ell!}{(n-k)!}\\
&=\binom{n-k}{k}\frac{(2k-n)_{\ell}}{(k-n)_{\ell}},\end{split}

(301)

where
(m)_{\ell}\coloneqq\prod_{j=0}^{n-1}(m+j)
is the rising Pochhammer symbol. We recognize that the other binomial coefficient can also be expressed using these Pochhammer symbols:

\binom{\ell+k-1}{k-1}=\frac{(k)_{\ell}}{\ell!}.

(302)

The sum can therefore be understood in terms of the Gauss hypergeometric function
{}_{2}F_{1}
:

\begin{split}\E_{u\sim\mathrm{Sym}(n)}\mathopen{}\left[3^{-\mathrm{loc}(P_{u(\bm{q})})}\right]\mathclose{}&=\binom{n}{2k}^{-1}\binom{n-k}{k}9^{-k}\sum_{\ell=0}^{n-2k}\frac{3^{-\ell}}{\ell!}\frac{(k)_{\ell}(2k-n)_{\ell}}{(k-n)_{\ell}}\\
&=\binom{n}{2k}^{-1}\binom{n-k}{k}9^{-k}\,{}_{2}F_{1}(k,2k-n;k-n;1/3).\end{split}

(303)

Using standard properties of the hypergeometric function, for
n
and
k
being positive integers and
k
a constant, we have the bounds
1\leq{}_{2}F_{1}(k,2k-n;k-n;1/3)\leq(3/2)^{k}
. In particular, this factor is independent of
n
.

We are ultimately interested in the lower bound of
Eq.

303
, since the shadow norm is its reciprocal. Then for
k=\mathcal{O}(1)
, we obtain

\|P_{\bm{q}}\|_{\mathrm{NC}}^{2}\leq\binom{n}{2k}\binom{n-k}{k}^{-1}9^{k}=\mathcal{O}(n^{k}).

(304)

Fermionic swap network bounds

In this section we describe another strategy to measure the
k
-RDM with almost optimal scaling in
n
. Generalizing from the measurement scheme introduced in Ref.
[
arute2020hartree
]
for 1-RDMs, this scheme employs fermionic swap gates to relabel which qubits correspond to which orbitals such that each
k
-RDM observable becomes a
2k
-local qubit observable. These local qubit observables may then be measured in a parallel fashion via Pauli measurements. As the circuit structure is equivalent, our
\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{NC}}
-based scheme may be viewed as a randomized version of this strategy.

For simplicity, we employ the Jordan–Wigner encoding throughout this section. The use of fermionic swap networks to minimize the qubit locality of fermionic operators was first utilized in the context of Hamiltonian simulation
[
kivlichan2018quantum
]
.

The 1-RDM method

We briefly describe the methods of Ref.
[
arute2020hartree
]
here. Consider estimating the 1-RDM elements
\tr(a_{p}^{\dagger}a_{q}\rho)
. The observables here are
\frac{1}{2}(a_{p}^{\dagger}a_{q}+\mathrm{h.c.})
and
\frac{1}{2\mathrm{i}}(a_{p}^{\dagger}a_{q}-\mathrm{h.c.})
, corresponding to the real and imaginary parts of the RDM element. In the experiment of Ref.
[
arute2020hartree
]
, they implement unitaries such that the imaginary part vanishes; for full generality, we will keep the imaginary parts. Diagonal elements are trivial to measure, since

a_{p}^{\dagger}a_{p}=\frac{I-Z_{p}}{2}.

(305)

The one-off-diagonal terms are precisely the local qubit operators we are interested in:

\displaystyle\frac{a_{p}^{\dagger}a_{p+1}+a_{p+1}^{\dagger}a_{p}}{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{X_{p}X_{p+1}+Y_{p}Y_{p+1}}{4},

(306)

\displaystyle\frac{a_{p}^{\dagger}a_{p+1}-a_{p+1}^{\dagger}a_{p}}{2\mathrm{i}}

\displaystyle=\frac{X_{p}Y_{p+1}-Y_{p}X_{p+1}}{4}.

(307)

Consider even and odd pairs of orbitals—even pairs being those starting with even indices, and analogously for the odd pairs. For the expectation values
\tr(a_{p}^{\dagger}a_{p+1}\rho)
on even pairs, we measure in 4 different bases:
X
on all qubits,
Y
on all qubits,
X
on every even qubit and
Y
on every odd qubit, and vice versa. Formally, the observables we measure are

\begin{split}O_{1}=\prod_{p=0}^{n-1}X_{p},\quad&O_{2}=\prod_{p=0}^{n-1}Y_{p}\\
O_{3}=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}p=0\\
\text{even}\end{subarray}}^{n-2}X_{p}Y_{p+1},\quad&O_{4}=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}p=0\\
\text{even}\end{subarray}}^{n-2}Y_{p}X_{p+1}.\end{split}

(308)

If
n
is odd, then we simply ignore the final
Y
(resp.
X
) in
O_{3}
(resp.
O_{4}
).

The remaining off-diagonal elements will incur Jordan–Wigner strings, making the Pauli operators highly nonlocal. To circumvent this, we perform fermionic swaps to relabel the indices such that we retain qubit locality. The fermionic swap gate between orbitals
p
and
q
is

\mathcal{F}_{pq}=\exp\mathopen{}\left[-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{2}(a_{p}^{\dagger}a_{q}+a_{q}^{\dagger}a_{p}-a_{p}^{\dagger}a_{p}-a_{q}^{\dagger}a_{q})\right]\mathclose{}.

(309)

This unitary is Gaussian and number-preserving, hence one may use the group homomorphism property to consolidate an arbitrarily large product of fermionic swaps into a single circuit of depth
n

[
kivlichan2018quantum
,
jiang2018quantum
]
.

As described in Ref.
[
kivlichan2018quantum
]
, a total of
\lceil n/2\rceil
different swap circuits are required to move orbitals such that every pair is nearest-neighbor at least once. This quantity can be understood from a simple counting argument: there are
\binom{n}{2}
off-diagonal 1-RDM elements (orbital pairs) to account for. Each ordering creates
n-1
nearest-neighbor pairs—therefore, we require
\binom{n}{2}/(n-1)=n/2
different orderings (hence unique swap circuits) to match all pairs of orbitals. In practice, this is achieved using a parallelized odd–even transposition sort
[
habermann1972parallel
]
.

Rounding up in the case that
n
is odd, and accounting for the four Pauli bases per permutation, the total number of different measurement circuits is
4\lceil n/2\rceil+1
.

The 2-RDM method

We now generalize the use of such swap networks for measuring
k
-RDMs. We will build up intuition with the 2-RDM. Diagonal terms are trivial as usual, since

a_{p}^{\dagger}a_{q}^{\dagger}a_{q}a_{p}=(1-\delta_{pq})\frac{I-Z_{p}-Z_{q}+Z_{p}Z_{q}}{4}.

(310)

The terms with a single occupation-number operator, restricted to 3-qubit locality, are

\displaystyle\frac{a_{p}^{\dagger}a_{q}^{\dagger}a_{q}a_{p+1}+\mathrm{h.c.}}{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{X_{p}X_{p+1}+Y_{p}Y_{p+1}-X_{p}X_{p+1}Z_{q}-Y_{p}Y_{p+1}Z_{q}}{8},

(311)

\displaystyle\frac{a_{p}^{\dagger}a_{q}^{\dagger}a_{q}a_{p+1}-\mathrm{h.c.}}{2\mathrm{i}}

\displaystyle=\frac{X_{p}Y_{p+1}-Y_{p}X_{p+1}-X_{p}Y_{p+1}Z_{q}+Y_{p}X_{p+1}Z_{q}}{8},

(312)

where
q\notin\{p,p+1\}
. Lastly, we have the most general case:

\displaystyle\frac{a_{p}^{\dagger}a_{p+1}^{\dagger}a_{q}a_{q+1}+\mathrm{h.c.}}{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{16}(-X_{p}X_{p+1}X_{q}X_{q+1}+X_{p}X_{p+1}Y_{q}Y_{q+1}

(313)

\displaystyle\qquad\ \ \,-X_{p}Y_{p+1}X_{q}Y_{q+1}-X_{p}Y_{p+1}Y_{q}X_{q+1}

\displaystyle\qquad\ \ \,-Y_{p}X_{p+1}X_{q}Y_{q+1}-Y_{p}X_{p+1}Y_{q}X_{q+1}

\displaystyle\qquad\ \ \,+Y_{p}Y_{p+1}X_{q}X_{q+1}-Y_{p}Y_{p+1}Y_{q}Y_{q+1}),

\displaystyle\frac{a_{p}^{\dagger}a_{p+1}^{\dagger}a_{q}a_{q+1}-\mathrm{h.c.}}{2\mathrm{i}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{16}(-X_{p}X_{p+1}X_{q}Y_{q+1}-X_{p}X_{p+1}Y_{q}X_{q+1}

(314)

\displaystyle\qquad\ \ \,+X_{p}Y_{p+1}X_{q}X_{q+1}-X_{p}Y_{p+1}Y_{q}Y_{q+1}

\displaystyle\qquad\ \ \,+Y_{p}X_{p+1}X_{q}X_{q+1}-Y_{p}X_{p+1}Y_{q}Y_{q+1}

\displaystyle\qquad\ \ \,+Y_{p}Y_{p+1}X_{q}Y_{q+1}+Y_{p}Y_{p+1}Y_{q}X_{q+1}).

The
a_{p}^{\dagger}a_{q}^{\dagger}a_{q+1}a_{p+1}
terms feature the same Pauli operators, but with a different sign pattern in the linear combination.

The 3- and 4-local terms are best handled separately. For the 3-local terms, the
q
th index is free to take any value different from
p
and
p+1
. We measure these qubits in the computational basis, and the
p
and
(p+1)
th qubits in the same fashion as in the 1-RDM case. Then to obtain all combinations (triples)
(p,p^{\prime},q)
, we have to swap all pairs
(p,p^{\prime})
into, say, the qubit ordering
(0,1)
. This allows a single ordering to account for
(n-2)
triples. There are
\binom{n}{2}(n-2)
total triples to permute into, and so we require
\binom{n}{2}
different swap circuits. To measure all 8 terms, we require 4 different Pauli bases, thus
4\binom{n}{2}
different measurement circuits. In practice we anticipate using the parallel transposition sort of the
1
-RDM measurement with an additional
n-1
measurements at each swap circuit to account for each
(p,p^{\prime},q)
triple as
n-2

(p,p^{\prime},q)
triples can be acquired simultaneously by measuring
p,p^{\prime}
in either
X
or
Y
and all other qubits in
Z
.

The 4-local terms require us to swap the orbital orderings into 2-combinations of pairs in order to measure all general
a_{p}^{\dagger}a_{p^{\prime}}^{\dagger}a_{q}a_{q^{\prime}}
terms. First, the number of nearest-neighbor pairs in
\{0,\ldots,n-1\}
is
n-1
. Then we count how many 2-combinations of these pairs we can construct. Note that locality between 2-combinations is not a constraint, since
p
and
q
do not have to be local. However, they must be disjoint, otherwise we are double counting the 3-local terms. Each swap circuit can account for
\binom{n-2}{2}
different pairs of pairs; thus, since there are
\binom{n}{2}\binom{n-2}{2}
unique
a_{p}^{\dagger}a_{p^{\prime}}^{\dagger}a_{q}a_{q^{\prime}}
terms, exactly
\binom{n}{2}
swap circuits are required. This statement is proved by a simple combinatorial argument, which we defer to the generalization in
A

k

-RDM generalization
.

The 16 Pauli operators cannot be measured in 16 bases whenever
n>7
. In particular, from Eq. (
313
) we observe that only the
XXXX
and
YYYY
geminals can be measured simultaneously for
n>7
. Therefore, in order to read off the 4-local Pauli observables, we rely on quantum overlap tomography (QOT)
[
cotler2020quantum
]
to provide asymptotic bounds. Each
(p,p^{\prime},q,q^{\prime})
term requires measuring almost all
4
-qubit marginals. More precisely, we need only the marginal elements corresponding to expectation values of Pauli operators composed of
X
and
Y
operators. QOT provides a bound for
4
-qubit bound that has
\mathcal{O}(\log(n))
scaling whenever the perfect hash family is known. Though large sets of the
(n,4)
-perfect hash families are documented, there remain significant gaps in
n
.

A more general procedure that does not achieve the same asymptotic bound of Ref.
[
cotler2020quantum
]
is the construction of a suboptimal perfect hash family by bootstrapping from the binary partitioning scheme described in Ref.
[
bonet2020nearly
]
. Using this approach, the leading order complexity, which upper bounds the true scaling, in the number of measurement settings is defined as the function EQOT (explicit quantum overlap tomography):

\mathrm{EQOT}(k,n)=3^{k}(k-1)\sum_{m=0}^{\lceil\log(n)\rceil-1}m^{k-2}.

(315)

This gives the number of partitions to measure all
k
-qubit marginals, multiplied by all
3^{k}
bases for each partition. In the case of the 4-local 2-RDM terms, however, we do not need to measure in any Pauli basis containing a
Z
, so we can straightforwardly reduce the base of this prefactor to
2
. Altogether, we can upper bound the number of circuit configurations as

M_{T}(\text{2-RDM})=1+4\binom{n}{2}+\binom{n}{2}\mathrm{EQOT}(4,n)\mathopen{}\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^{4}\mathclose{}

(316)

circuits to measure the full fermionic 2-RDM with this approach. Again we emphasize that, though this
\mathrm{EQOT}(4,n)
scaling is not optimal, it will work for any
n
. In the following section we prove the partition scaling for the component of the
k
-RDM with
2k
distinct indices, and thus the complexity, given an optimal construction of the swap circuits.

A
k
-RDM generalization

Each
k
-RDM observable decomposes into up to
4^{k}
Pauli operators, thus is it impractical to write out such terms for general
k
by hand. Nonetheless, we can still obtain a scaling estimate on how many measurement circuits are required to reach all
k
-RDM elements. The following argument also applies for the 4-local terms of the 2-RDM in the previous section. Consider the asymptotically dominant terms,
a_{p_{1}}^{\dagger}\cdots a_{p_{k}}^{\dagger}a_{p_{k}+1}\cdots a_{p_{2k}}
where all
p_{i}\neq p_{j}
for
i\neq j
. There are
\binom{n}{k}\binom{n-k}{k}
such index combinations. The number of
k
-combinations of disjoint nearest-neighbor pairs taken from
\{(0,1),(1,2),\ldots,(n-2,n-1)\}
is equivalent to counting how many unique sets of
k
nonconsecutive integers from
\{0,\ldots,n-2\}
exist. This is a classic “stars and bars” combinatorial problem and has solution
\binom{(n-1)-k+1}{k}=\binom{n-k}{k}
. We can see this by a visual argument: write down
(n-1)-k
spaces where the unchosen numbers will be placed in order. Then there are
(n-1)-k-1
gaps in between the spaces, plus the
2
endpoints, where the chosen numbers can be placed, of which we choose
k
. Thus we require
\binom{n}{k}
different swap circuits, hence
\Omega\mathopen{}\left[\binom{n}{k}4^{k}\right]\mathclose{}
unique measurement circuits, to reach all
k
-RDM elements. Similar to the 2-RDM case, this lower bound is in general not achievable, as we require (E)QOT to measure the Pauli operators in parallel, thus incurring polylogarithmic factors. We can upper bound this scaling by counting circuit repetitions for each
k
-RDM element partitioned by the number of unique indices.

Upper bounds for
k=3,4

To derive an upper bound for arbitrary
n
in terms of measurement configurations for the 3-RDM and 4-RDM, we can follow the same procedure as the 2-RDM: count circuits for measuring RDM terms after partitioning based on the number of unique indices in each RDM element. The 3-RDM is partitioned into terms with 3, 4, 5, 6 different indices. This can be checked by building a basis for the unique 3-RDM elements indexed by a tuple
(p,q,r)
with
p<q<r
. The terms with only 3 unique indices are analogous to the 2-index case for the 2-RDM—e.g., the Jordan–Wigner transformation of the three index term corresponds to diagonal 3-RDM elements and thus involves only Pauli-
Z
operators, as follows. All three index terms are of the form

a_{p}^{\dagger}a_{q}^{\dagger}a_{r}^{\dagger}a_{r}a_{q}a_{p}=\frac{-Z_{p}-Z_{q}-Z_{r}+Z_{p}Z_{q}+Z_{p}Z_{r}+Z_{q}Z_{r}-Z_{p}Z_{q}Z_{r}}{8}

(317)

and can be measured in one permutation of qubits (the identity permutation).

The terms with four unique indices are similar to the 3-index 2-RDM case. For example, consider the real component of a 4-index 3-RDM term,

a_{p}^{\dagger}a_{q}^{\dagger}a_{r}^{\dagger}a_{r}a_{q}a_{p+1}+\mathrm{h.c.}=\frac{1}{8}[X_{p}X_{p+1}+Y_{p}Y_{p+1}+(X_{p}X_{p+1}+Y_{p}Y_{p+1})(Z_{q}Z_{r}-Z_{q}-Z_{r})].

(318)

These terms can be measured in
n/2
circuits for the
XX
and
YY
parts, followed by
\binom{n}{2}
circuits for the
XXZ
and
YYZ
terms, using the fact that
XX+YY
commutes with
ZZ
, allowing us to use the measurement circuit from Ref.
[
arute2020hartree
]
. At each of the
n/2
circuit configurations, all
n-1
pairs must account for all other
Z
operators. Thus in total, counting real and imaginary parts, we have a total of
4\binom{n}{2}
qubit permutations to measure all 4-index terms.

The 5-index terms contain one
Z
term and can be measured with swap circuits analogous to the 4-index case in the 2-RDM. Consider the example of the real-component of the 5-index 3-RDM term,

a_{p}^{\dagger}a_{q}^{\dagger}a_{r}^{\dagger}a_{r}a_{q+1}a_{p+1}+a_{p+1}^{\dagger}a_{q+1}^{\dagger}a_{r}^{\dagger}a_{r}a_{q}a_{p}=\frac{1}{16}\left(A_{pq}+Z_{r}A_{pq}\right),

(319)

where we define

\begin{split}A_{pq}&\equiv(X_{p}X_{p+1}X_{q}X_{q+1}+X_{p}X_{p+1}Y_{q}Y_{q+1}+X_{p}Y_{p+1}X_{q}Y_{q+1}-X_{p}Y_{p+1}Y_{q}X_{q+1}\\
&\quad+Y_{p}X_{p+1}Y_{q}X_{q+1}-Y_{p}X_{p+1}X_{q}Y_{q+1}+Y_{p}Y_{p+1}X_{q}X_{q+1}+Y_{p}Y_{p+1}Y_{q}Y_{q+1}),\end{split}

(320)

with
r\notin\{p,p+1,q,q+1\}
. We can obtain an upper bound for the number of unique measurements settings as
\binom{n}{k}M
, where
M
is the complexity of measuring
5
-qubit marginals, again using the technique of Ref.
[
bonet2020nearly
]
. The scaling for
M
is given in Eq. (
315
) for
k=5
and has a prefactor of
3^{5}
to measure all
X
,
Y
, and
Z
terms. Finally, the 6-index term involves 64 separate terms, including the imaginary terms, which is measured by constructing the
\binom{n}{k}
swap circuits and using EQOT for each permutation on the
6
-qubit marginal terms which make up the 3-RDM element.

Figure 2:
Scaling of the number of measurement configurations needed while measuring the
k
-RDM, for
k=2,3,4
(blue, yellow, green), via the swap network protocol combined with quantum overlap tomography (solid lines) compared against the protocol where the unique upper triangle of the supermatrix representing the
k
-RDM is measured. For the naive measurement strategy we scale the unique number of terms in the
k
-RDM by
3^{2k}
which is obtained by counting products of
\sigma^{+},\sigma^{-},Z
. A constant-factor improvement is possible by taking advantage of
S_{z}
-spin symmetry and only considering the spin-adapted blocks of the RDM supermatrix
[
rubin2018application
]
. The swap protocol upper bound is a quadratic improvement over the naive scaling. The solid curves corresponding to the SWAP+EQOT protocol are equivalent to the orange points in Fig.
1
of the main text.

Overall, for the 3-RDM we can loosely bound the number of measurements as

M_{T}(\text{3-RDM})=1+4\binom{n}{2}+\binom{n}{2}\mathrm{EQOT}(5)+\binom{n}{3}\mathrm{EQOT}(6,n)\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^{6},

(321)

where each term corresponds to measuring the 3-, 4-, 5-, and 6-index terms of the 3-RDM, respectively. We note that this is an overestimate since Eq. (
315
) provides an upper bound to the
k
-qubit marginal measurement. It is further loosened by the fact that we clearly do not need to measure
all

k
-qubit marginal terms.

A similar accounting can be performed for the 4-RDM by breaking the unique 4-RDM elements into sets consisting of terms with 4, 5, 6, 7, and 8 unique indices. This gives the upper bound on measurement configurations as

M_{T}(\text{4-RDM})=1+4\binom{n}{2}+\binom{n}{2}\mathrm{EQOT}(6,n)+\binom{n}{3}\mathrm{EQOT}(7,n)+\binom{n}{4}\mathrm{EQOT}(8,n)\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^{8}.

(322)

In
Fig.

2
we plot the scaling of the swap network EQOT protocol against naively measuring the upper triangle of unique
k
-RDM elements in a supermatrix representation, as a function of the number of fermionic modes.

Supplementary numerical calculations

Here we provide some additional findings with our numerical studies. These are not essential to the primary results of the main text, but rather serve to explore some of the more subtler points of our partial tomography scheme.

Hyperparameter tuning

As mentioned in the main text, we may control how many circuits to randomly generate by setting a hyperparameter
r
such that all
K_{r}
unitaries correspond to measurements of all observables at least
r
times each. Since increasing the sample size decreases the frequency of outlier events (i.e., some subset of observables being accounted for more often than the rest), we then expect that the value of
K_{r}/r
decrease as a function of
r
. Indeed, this is a generic feature of randomization, as observed in the numerical results of the original work on classical shadows
[
huang2020predicting
]
. This effect is demonstrated, with the 2-RDM as an example, in
Fig.

3
. The behavior is consistent as expected.

Figure 3:
The relation between
K_{r}/r
and
r
for various numbers of modes under the
\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{FGU}}
ensemble, with respect to covering
2
-RDM observables. Since
K_{r}
is a random variable, we average over 10 randomly generated circuit collections for each
r
and indicate 1 standard deviation with uncertainty bars.

Realistic time estimates

The main experimental difference between deterministic and randomized measurement schemes is the number of unique circuits one must run. In general, the number of random unitaries (
K_{r}
) will be larger than the deterministic clique cover size (
C
). Thus depending on the architecture, reprogramming the quantum device for each new circuit may incur a nontrivial overhead in the actual wall-clock time of the algorithm. Here, we show that for realistic experimental parameters, this consideration does not affect the results presented in the main text.

Suppose the quantum device can repeatedly sample a fixed circuit at a rate
f_{\mathrm{samp}}
, but requires time
t_{\mathrm{load}}
to load a new circuit. Then the total measurement time under the two paradigms are

\displaystyle T_{\mathrm{deter}}

\displaystyle=C\mathopen{}\left(\frac{S}{f_{\mathrm{samp}}}+t_{\mathrm{load}}\right)\mathclose{},

(323)

\displaystyle T_{\mathrm{rand}}

\displaystyle=K_{r}\mathopen{}\left(\frac{\lceil S/r\rceil}{f_{\mathrm{samp}}}+t_{\mathrm{load}}\right)\mathclose{},

(324)

where we recall that
S=\mathcal{O}(1/\varepsilon^{2})
. Note that in the regime where
S\gg f_{\mathrm{samp}}t_{\mathrm{load}}
, we may directly compare
C
to
K_{r}/r
, as in the main text. For hardware-dependent estimates, we take the specifications of the Google Sycamore chip as an example
[
arute2019quantum
,
arute2020hartree
,
harrigan2021quantum
,
arute2020observation
]
. The reported parameter values are
f_{\mathrm{samp}}=5\times 10^{3}
Hz and
t_{\mathrm{load}}=0.1
s
[
sung2020exploration
]
, and for
Fig.

4
, we set
S=2.5\times 10^{5}
, in line with the number of shots taken to estimate 1-RDM elements in a recent Hartree–Fock experiment
[
arute2020hartree
]
. We observe no qualitative differences from the results of the main text.

Figure 4:
Measurement times for estimating
k
-RDMs, calculated with
Eqs.

323
and
324
, under the reported device parameters of the Google Sycamore chip
[
sung2020exploration
]
and with
S=2.5\times 10^{5}
. The underlying data is that of
Fig.

1
in the main text. For convenience, we reproduce the legend of the main text here.

One may also study the performance of the different methods as a function of the target accuracy. In
Fig.

5
, we show how
T_{\mathrm{rand}}
scales with
S
for the 2-RDM, using a few values of
n
as illustrative examples. Though the threshold at which randomization begins to outperform the other methods varies depending on
n
and
k
, it typically lies below
{\sim}\,10^{5}
, which is well under typical sampling requirements. Note that the
S\gg f_{\mathrm{samp}}t_{\mathrm{load}}
regime corresponds to when
T_{\mathrm{rand}}
scales linearly with
S
.

Figure 5:
Examples of how the measurement times for our randomized schemes scale with the level of precision, as given by
Eq.

324
. We use the same device parameters here as in
Fig.

4
. For comparison, we also plot the linear scaling of the prior deterministic strategies (excluding the swap network method), which gives an indication for the values of
S
beyond which we obtain an advantage with our methods under this time-cost model. The colors correspond to those in the legend of
Fig.

4
.

Hamiltonian averaging

In the context of estimating a single observable, whose expectation value is a linear combination of RDM elements, the number of circuit repetitions required is more properly determined by taking into account the coefficients of the terms and covariances between simultaneously measured terms
[
wecker2015progress
,
mcclean2016theory
,
rubin2018application
]
. This can be directly calculated from the (single-shot) variance of the corresponding estimator. Here we provide some preliminary numerical calculations in this context, with respect to
\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{FGU}}
. Although our uniformly distributed ensemble is not tailored for Hamiltonian averaging, these calculations provide a benchmark for potential improvement.

Without loss of generality, consider a traceless fermionic
k
-body Hamiltonian

H=\sum_{j=1}^{k}\sum_{\bm{\mu}\in\mathcal{C}_{2n,2j}}h_{\bm{\mu}}\Gamma_{\bm{\mu}},\quad h_{\bm{\mu}}\in\mathbb{R}.

(325)

For the numerical studies presented here, we consider a sample of molecular Hamiltonians (hence
k=2
) at equilibrium nuclear geometry, obtained through OpenFermion
[
openfermion
]
interfaced with the Psi4 electronic structure package
[
psi4
]
. We used a minimal STO-3G orbital basis set to generate these Hamiltonians, except for the
\mathrm{H}_{2}
molecule, which was represented in the 6-31G basis.

In Table
1
we compare our classical shadows (CS)
\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{FGU}}
ensemble against two prominent measurement schemes for electronic-structure Hamiltonians: a strategy based on a low-rank factorization of the coefficient tensor, termed basis-rotation grouping (BRG)
[
huggins2021efficient
]
, and a locally biased adaptation on classical shadows (LBCS)
[
hadfield2022measurements
]
. For reference, we also report the variance under standard classical shadows with Pauli measurements
[
huang2020predicting
]
. The expressions for the variances in terms of the Hamiltonian terms and a reference state
\rho
(taken here to be the ground state) are provided in their respective references. While we provide a state-independent upper bound in
Variance bounds for arbitrary observables
, the exact variance expression for our
\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{FGU}}
ensemble on arbitrary observables has been derived in Refs.
[
wan2023matchgate
,
ogorman2022fermionic
]
.
